Formalizing US Tax Code (Using Lean)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:41:20 UTC
00:41:20 KST

Formalizing the US tax code
Yesterday, we verified that known tax loopholes could be identified using a Lean search. Today, we set out to formalize the entire Internal Revenue Code to hunt for unknown loopholes (bugs).
Pointing a proof checker at statutes
Lean is a programming language whose type checker can also check mathematical proofs: if Lean accepts a proof, every step follows from the definitions, with nothing taken on trust. An earlier post discussed how far that kind of formalisation can reach in law,[1] and yesterday we used it on the retirement-account rules, where a blind search rediscovered the backdoor Roth.[2]
This work is part of global-law, a CC0 repository of versioned legislation and case law.[3] Our argument is that pointing AI and a proof checker at a versioned record of the law finds the contradictions and mechanical mistakes that people miss. In particular, we can catch mistakes in the text, and see what the rules allow, including what they allow by accident. To do so, we translated each section of the law into Lean, staying close to the statute’s own words, and tested it against the worked examples the IRS prints in its forms and instructions.
1. The tax law’s text has 138 drafting mistakes
The tax law is Title 26 of the US Code. Its official text is published as structured data by the Office of the Law Revision Counsel, the office of the House of Representatives that compiles the Code.[4] We used the release current to 3 November 2026.
A parser then reads every cross-reference, including the ones written in words (“paragraph (2) of subsection (b)”, “such subparagraph”, “sections 922 through 927”), which carry no hyperlink. Lean checks each one. Does the provision it points to exist? Is it still in force? (verifiable via our versioned history) The checks run as a test suite, which fails whenever a new release adds a fault the last one did not have.
The suite reports 257 findings, each checked by an AI agent against the official text and its editorial notes: 98 intended (rules for past periods, and provisions enacted but not yet in effect), 21 false alarms in the editors’ own hyperlinks, and 138 real faults:
Each of the 138 has a certificate: a short Lean statement that carries its evidence from the official XML and proves the fault. For a dead reference, the evidence is the full list of what does exist under the cited provision. Lean re-checks all 138 in about two and a half minutes.
Our record of the US Code is a git history, one commit per public law,[5] so each fault can be traced to the law that wrote it. For Title 26 the record reaches back to the 1994 edition.
This check is cheap to repeat. Run against each new release of the Code, it flags a new mistake as soon as it appears, and names the law that caused it.
2. Rewriting the income tax as code that can be checked
Next we wrote down what the law calculates: the 2025 federal income tax for one household, meaning the numbers on Form 1040, including the changes made by the One Big Beautiful Bill Act in July 2025.[6],[7] We did it in four parts:
- Income, down to adjusted gross income (AGI). AGI is total income minus a few items, such as IRA contributions and student-loan interest. Most other tax rules depend on it.
- Deductions: the standard deduction or itemized deductions, and the new deductions for tips, overtime, car-loan interest and people over 65.
- The tax itself, including the alternative minimum tax (AMT), a second calculation that stops high earners from cutting their tax too far with deductions.
- Credits, such as the child tax credit and the earned income tax credit (EITC), a credit for people on low and moderate wages.
We used AI to translate the statute into Lean. This is the lossy step, therefore we reinforce it with the following measures:
- Each part is tested against figures the IRS publishes (the Tax Table, the EITC table, the worksheets and the examples in its publications), and checked against three independent tax programs on 5,000 households (section 3).
- A second agent, which did not write that part, reviews it adversarially, looking for errors and for cheating, such as code written to pass the tests rather than to follow the statute.
What Lean ultimately adds is proofs that cover every input instead of a sample. For example, it proves that the SALT cap and the car-loan and overtime deductions never grow when income rises, at every income and for every filing status.
The comparison with other tax software helps us catch logical errors. That is how we caught two mistakes in our own model. It is also how we found bugs in the other tools that were logical rather than arithmetic: Tax-Calculator gives the EITC to separate filers without checking whether the spouses lived apart, and UsTaxes never attaches Schedule C to the 2025 return, so self-employment income disappears.
Writing the model turned up five things:
- The published Code mixes 2025 and 2026 law. In six places the consolidated Code already shows the 2025 Act’s amendments that start in 2026. Read without the effective-date notes, it gives the wrong 2025 tax: the AMT phase-out at 50% instead of 25%, a charity floor and a cap on itemized deductions that do not yet apply.
- The same Act rounds in opposite directions. The new tips and overtime deductions shrink by $100 for each full $1,000 of income over the limit, so one more dollar of income can cost $100 of deduction. The new car-loan deduction shrinks by $200 for each $1,000 “or portion thereof”, which rounds the other way, so it runs out $999 sooner than the round numbers suggest.
- A married couple can deduct more state and local tax by filing separately. The deduction for state and local taxes (SALT) is capped, and the cap shrinks as income rises: by 30 cents for each dollar over $500,000 on a joint return. On a separate return, the law halves the cap after shrinking it, so it shrinks by only 15 cents a dollar. A couple earning $300,000 each can deduct up to $25,000 on two separate returns, but only $10,000 on one joint return.
- The Tax Table is not the tax formula. Most people with taxable income under $100,000 look up their tax in the IRS Tax Table instead of working it out. The table differs from the formula in the law by between −$5 and +$6, and Lean proves that range for every income under $100,000 and every filing status.
- One dollar can cost $8,046. Some benefits switch off at a line instead of fading out. A family with three children can lose its whole EITC, up to $8,046, because its investment income is one dollar over $11,950. The saver’s credit, a credit for retirement saving, works the same way: a couple who each put $2,000 into retirement accounts gets $2,000 at an income of $47,500, and $800 at $47,501.
3. Testing it against tax software people already use
A model is only useful if it gives the right answers. To check ours, we built 5,000 sample households and worked out each one’s 2025 tax four ways: with our model, and with three tools built by other teams, independently of each other and of us:
- PolicyEngine-US, an open-source model that researchers and policymakers use to estimate how tax and benefit changes affect households;[8]
- Tax-Calculator, the Policy Simulation Library’s open-source model of the federal income tax;[9]
- UsTaxes, a free, open-source app for preparing your own tax return.[10]
Two thousand households were ordinary. The other 3,000 were deliberately awkward: couples filing separately, widows and widowers, students, people with tips or overtime, IRAs and health savings accounts, education and saver’s credits, and incomes over $1 million with large investment gains.
Our model and PolicyEngine-US agreed on every figure for 4,993 of the 5,000 households. The other seven differ by at most $11, all in the EITC: our model reads the IRS’s printed EITC table, while PolicyEngine-US uses the formula behind it.[11]
Tax-Calculator gave the EITC and the child and dependent care credit to married people filing separately without checking that the spouses lived apart, which the law requires. It also computed no AMT for one high-earning separate filer, where two other calculators computed $14,881.
UsTaxes had 14 bugs, each confirmed by recalculating an affected household. It drops self-employment income without a warning, because its 2025 return never attaches Schedule C, the form for business income. Its child-care credit never reaches the return. Its age test for the EITC always passes. And its self-employment tax counts a spouse’s wages against the cap on Social Security tax. Together, these bugs affect about one ordinary household in six.
The comparison caught two mistakes in our own model too. We had not counted self-employment profit toward the 20% deduction for small-business income. And we always took the larger of the standard and itemized deductions, although the law lets you choose to itemize a smaller amount, which can pay when it lowers the AMT. With that fixed, one household owed $2,070 less. Both mistakes showed up because two independent tools agreed with each other and not with us.
4. A search found the loopholes tax advisers already use
With a model that matches the reference software, we used it as we had used the retirement rules: as the oracle for a search that knows no tax planning. The first search tried ordinary choices for each of the 5,000 households, 195,787 returns in all:
- file separately, and which spouse holds which investments, pays which bills and claims which children;
- contribute to an IRA;
- earn a little less;
- realize more or less gain.
The second search used a multi-year family model, which adds the kiddie tax, trust taxation, Roth conversions, required withdrawals, gifts and death. It tried about 30,000 plans for four families over 34 to 47 years. Everything large that the two searches found is already in the planning literature:
As with the backdoor Roth, none of these was described to the search. It found them because the rules, written down plainly, permit them.
5. Novel loopholes we found
Three results did not turn up in practitioner sources, IRS guidance or tax-software forums.
Filing separately can cut tax on Social Security, even for a couple living together. The standard advice is that a couple living together should never file separately when one draws Social Security.[16] A separate filer who lived with their spouse gets no base amount at all. But taxable benefits are capped at the lesser of 85% of benefits or 85% of provisional income,[17],[18] and the search found couples for whom that cap makes separate returns cheaper. On a joint return the working spouse’s wage counts in provisional income, so a moderate wage already pushes the benefits to the 85% maximum. On a separate return the retiree’s benefits are measured against the retiree’s income alone. With $48,000 of benefits and a $60,000 wage, the couple owes $7,050 jointly and $5,568 separately, $1,482 less. A second reviewer recomputed it by hand and got the same answer. At a $100,000 wage the result flips, so the window is real but narrow.
Wages on one return, investments on the other. The 3.8% tax on investment income applies to the smaller of the investment income and the excess of income over $250,000 jointly, or $125,000 on a separate return.[19] On a joint return, wages use up that threshold first. On separate returns the investing spouse’s income excludes the other’s wages, and their gains start in their own 0% band. With about $200,000 of each, that saves $1,621. Every explainer gives the threshold. We found none that describes the split or where it pays.
Schedule 8812 pays more than the statute allows. This is a gap between the IRS form and the law, not a plan. The statute caps the refundable child credit per qualifying child, computed without the $500 credit for other dependents. The form caps the combined credit instead, so in the phase-out range, with no tax left to absorb it, it refunds the $500 credits in cash.[20],[21] Example: one child and one other dependent, $240,000 of income, no tax left. The statute gives $200, the form $700. Lean proves the form is never below the statute and agrees with it exactly when there are no other dependents.
6. Why one year of the tax code holds no big loopholes
The backdoor Roth is big because two provisions combine to remove an income limit entirely, and the money then compounds tax-free for decades. A single year of the individual income tax is full of cliffs and per-return thresholds, but each is bounded: the largest swing we found was a few thousand dollars. Over decades, the large gains come from moves that are already published. The one combination we could not find written up was paying the children in the same years as Roth conversions, which adds about 0.5%. Our guess is that anything Roth-sized and unpublished lives where value moves between entities and their owners: partnerships, S corporations and C corporations.
7. Most of this can now run automatically
“The long-term problem is how this kind of bug-hunting can be done routinely, e.g. as part of a test suite. Some approaches aren’t really automatable, because they require a lot of both human and AI agent work.”
What still needs a person is deciding what a finding means. Some cliffs are deliberate. Others are in the IRS’s forms, not in the law, whilst others come from the way the official text is compiled, yet:
- The text checks are routine. A new release is one download and one command, which fails on any fault the last release did not have, and git blame names the Act responsible.
- The model is a one-off cost. Once written and proved, it is a build: if a later change breaks a proved property, the build fails. When the law changes, only the changed provisions need rewriting.
- The comparison is routine once the input mappings are right. Getting them right was the hard part, and the same comparison found every mapping error.
Limits
- Coverage. The model covers the 2025 individual income tax for a household record. It leaves out the kiddie tax on the single-year return, 28% and §1250 gain, foreign income, passive activity rules, and most AMT preferences. The multi-year model holds 2025 law constant.
- Who checked what. The drafting triage and the reviews were done by AI agents against the official text, and have not been checked by a lawyer or CPA.
- Agreement is not truth. Where all four calculators agree they may share a misreading. The theorems and the IRS-example tests are the check on the model itself.
The code, the certificates, the comparison and the searches are in the lean-proofs folder of global-law.[3]
References

- Internal Revenue Service, Rev Proc 2024-40 <www.irs.gov/pub/irs-drop/rp-24-40.pdf>
- An Act to provide for reconciliation pursuant to title II of H. Con. Res. 14, Pub L 119-21 (4 July 2025) <www.congress.gov/bill/119th-congress/house-bill/1>
- PolicyEngine, policyengine-us (version 2.116.9) <github.com/PolicyEngine/policyengine-us>
- Policy Simulation Library, Tax-Calculator (version 6.8.5) <github.com/PSLmodels/Tax-Calculator>

Formalizing US Tax Code (Using Lean)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:41:20 UTC
00:41:20 KST

Formalizing the US tax code
Yesterday, we verified that known tax loopholes could be identified using a Lean search. Today, we set out to formalize the entire Internal Revenue Code to hunt for unknown loopholes (bugs).
Pointing a proof checker at statutes
Lean is a programming language whose type checker can also check mathematical proofs: if Lean accepts a proof, every step follows from the definitions, with nothing taken on trust. An earlier post discussed how far that kind of formalisation can reach in law,[1] and yesterday we used it on the retirement-account rules, where a blind search rediscovered the backdoor Roth.[2]
This work is part of global-law, a CC0 repository of versioned legislation and case law.[3] Our argument is that pointing AI and a proof checker at a versioned record of the law finds the contradictions and mechanical mistakes that people miss. In particular, we can catch mistakes in the text, and see what the rules allow, including what they allow by accident. To do so, we translated each section of the law into Lean, staying close to the statute’s own words, and tested it against the worked examples the IRS prints in its forms and instructions.
1. The tax law’s text has 138 drafting mistakes
The tax law is Title 26 of the US Code. Its official text is published as structured data by the Office of the Law Revision Counsel, the office of the House of Representatives that compiles the Code.[4] We used the release current to 3 November 2026.
A parser then reads every cross-reference, including the ones written in words (“paragraph (2) of subsection (b)”, “such subparagraph”, “sections 922 through 927”), which carry no hyperlink. Lean checks each one. Does the provision it points to exist? Is it still in force? (verifiable via our versioned history) The checks run as a test suite, which fails whenever a new release adds a fault the last one did not have.
The suite reports 257 findings, each checked by an AI agent against the official text and its editorial notes: 98 intended (rules for past periods, and provisions enacted but not yet in effect), 21 false alarms in the editors’ own hyperlinks, and 138 real faults:
Each of the 138 has a certificate: a short Lean statement that carries its evidence from the official XML and proves the fault. For a dead reference, the evidence is the full list of what does exist under the cited provision. Lean re-checks all 138 in about two and a half minutes.
Our record of the US Code is a git history, one commit per public law,[5] so each fault can be traced to the law that wrote it. For Title 26 the record reaches back to the 1994 edition.
This check is cheap to repeat. Run against each new release of the Code, it flags a new mistake as soon as it appears, and names the law that caused it.
2. Rewriting the income tax as code that can be checked
Next we wrote down what the law calculates: the 2025 federal income tax for one household, meaning the numbers on Form 1040, including the changes made by the One Big Beautiful Bill Act in July 2025.[6],[7] We did it in four parts:
- Income, down to adjusted gross income (AGI). AGI is total income minus a few items, such as IRA contributions and student-loan interest. Most other tax rules depend on it.
- Deductions: the standard deduction or itemized deductions, and the new deductions for tips, overtime, car-loan interest and people over 65.
- The tax itself, including the alternative minimum tax (AMT), a second calculation that stops high earners from cutting their tax too far with deductions.
- Credits, such as the child tax credit and the earned income tax credit (EITC), a credit for people on low and moderate wages.
We used AI to translate the statute into Lean. This is the lossy step, therefore we reinforce it with the following measures:
- Each part is tested against figures the IRS publishes (the Tax Table, the EITC table, the worksheets and the examples in its publications), and checked against three independent tax programs on 5,000 households (section 3).
- A second agent, which did not write that part, reviews it adversarially, looking for errors and for cheating, such as code written to pass the tests rather than to follow the statute.
What Lean ultimately adds is proofs that cover every input instead of a sample. For example, it proves that the SALT cap and the car-loan and overtime deductions never grow when income rises, at every income and for every filing status.
The comparison with other tax software helps us catch logical errors. That is how we caught two mistakes in our own model. It is also how we found bugs in the other tools that were logical rather than arithmetic: Tax-Calculator gives the EITC to separate filers without checking whether the spouses lived apart, and UsTaxes never attaches Schedule C to the 2025 return, so self-employment income disappears.
Writing the model turned up five things:
- The published Code mixes 2025 and 2026 law. In six places the consolidated Code already shows the 2025 Act’s amendments that start in 2026. Read without the effective-date notes, it gives the wrong 2025 tax: the AMT phase-out at 50% instead of 25%, a charity floor and a cap on itemized deductions that do not yet apply.
- The same Act rounds in opposite directions. The new tips and overtime deductions shrink by $100 for each full $1,000 of income over the limit, so one more dollar of income can cost $100 of deduction. The new car-loan deduction shrinks by $200 for each $1,000 “or portion thereof”, which rounds the other way, so it runs out $999 sooner than the round numbers suggest.
- A married couple can deduct more state and local tax by filing separately. The deduction for state and local taxes (SALT) is capped, and the cap shrinks as income rises: by 30 cents for each dollar over $500,000 on a joint return. On a separate return, the law halves the cap after shrinking it, so it shrinks by only 15 cents a dollar. A couple earning $300,000 each can deduct up to $25,000 on two separate returns, but only $10,000 on one joint return.
- The Tax Table is not the tax formula. Most people with taxable income under $100,000 look up their tax in the IRS Tax Table instead of working it out. The table differs from the formula in the law by between −$5 and +$6, and Lean proves that range for every income under $100,000 and every filing status.
- One dollar can cost $8,046. Some benefits switch off at a line instead of fading out. A family with three children can lose its whole EITC, up to $8,046, because its investment income is one dollar over $11,950. The saver’s credit, a credit for retirement saving, works the same way: a couple who each put $2,000 into retirement accounts gets $2,000 at an income of $47,500, and $800 at $47,501.
3. Testing it against tax software people already use
A model is only useful if it gives the right answers. To check ours, we built 5,000 sample households and worked out each one’s 2025 tax four ways: with our model, and with three tools built by other teams, independently of each other and of us:
- PolicyEngine-US, an open-source model that researchers and policymakers use to estimate how tax and benefit changes affect households;[8]
- Tax-Calculator, the Policy Simulation Library’s open-source model of the federal income tax;[9]
- UsTaxes, a free, open-source app for preparing your own tax return.[10]
Two thousand households were ordinary. The other 3,000 were deliberately awkward: couples filing separately, widows and widowers, students, people with tips or overtime, IRAs and health savings accounts, education and saver’s credits, and incomes over $1 million with large investment gains.
Our model and PolicyEngine-US agreed on every figure for 4,993 of the 5,000 households. The other seven differ by at most $11, all in the EITC: our model reads the IRS’s printed EITC table, while PolicyEngine-US uses the formula behind it.[11]
Tax-Calculator gave the EITC and the child and dependent care credit to married people filing separately without checking that the spouses lived apart, which the law requires. It also computed no AMT for one high-earning separate filer, where two other calculators computed $14,881.
UsTaxes had 14 bugs, each confirmed by recalculating an affected household. It drops self-employment income without a warning, because its 2025 return never attaches Schedule C, the form for business income. Its child-care credit never reaches the return. Its age test for the EITC always passes. And its self-employment tax counts a spouse’s wages against the cap on Social Security tax. Together, these bugs affect about one ordinary household in six.
The comparison caught two mistakes in our own model too. We had not counted self-employment profit toward the 20% deduction for small-business income. And we always took the larger of the standard and itemized deductions, although the law lets you choose to itemize a smaller amount, which can pay when it lowers the AMT. With that fixed, one household owed $2,070 less. Both mistakes showed up because two independent tools agreed with each other and not with us.
4. A search found the loopholes tax advisers already use
With a model that matches the reference software, we used it as we had used the retirement rules: as the oracle for a search that knows no tax planning. The first search tried ordinary choices for each of the 5,000 households, 195,787 returns in all:
- file separately, and which spouse holds which investments, pays which bills and claims which children;
- contribute to an IRA;
- earn a little less;
- realize more or less gain.
The second search used a multi-year family model, which adds the kiddie tax, trust taxation, Roth conversions, required withdrawals, gifts and death. It tried about 30,000 plans for four families over 34 to 47 years. Everything large that the two searches found is already in the planning literature:
As with the backdoor Roth, none of these was described to the search. It found them because the rules, written down plainly, permit them.
5. Novel loopholes we found
Three results did not turn up in practitioner sources, IRS guidance or tax-software forums.
Filing separately can cut tax on Social Security, even for a couple living together. The standard advice is that a couple living together should never file separately when one draws Social Security.[16] A separate filer who lived with their spouse gets no base amount at all. But taxable benefits are capped at the lesser of 85% of benefits or 85% of provisional income,[17],[18] and the search found couples for whom that cap makes separate returns cheaper. On a joint return the working spouse’s wage counts in provisional income, so a moderate wage already pushes the benefits to the 85% maximum. On a separate return the retiree’s benefits are measured against the retiree’s income alone. With $48,000 of benefits and a $60,000 wage, the couple owes $7,050 jointly and $5,568 separately, $1,482 less. A second reviewer recomputed it by hand and got the same answer. At a $100,000 wage the result flips, so the window is real but narrow.
Wages on one return, investments on the other. The 3.8% tax on investment income applies to the smaller of the investment income and the excess of income over $250,000 jointly, or $125,000 on a separate return.[19] On a joint return, wages use up that threshold first. On separate returns the investing spouse’s income excludes the other’s wages, and their gains start in their own 0% band. With about $200,000 of each, that saves $1,621. Every explainer gives the threshold. We found none that describes the split or where it pays.
Schedule 8812 pays more than the statute allows. This is a gap between the IRS form and the law, not a plan. The statute caps the refundable child credit per qualifying child, computed without the $500 credit for other dependents. The form caps the combined credit instead, so in the phase-out range, with no tax left to absorb it, it refunds the $500 credits in cash.[20],[21] Example: one child and one other dependent, $240,000 of income, no tax left. The statute gives $200, the form $700. Lean proves the form is never below the statute and agrees with it exactly when there are no other dependents.
6. Why one year of the tax code holds no big loopholes
The backdoor Roth is big because two provisions combine to remove an income limit entirely, and the money then compounds tax-free for decades. A single year of the individual income tax is full of cliffs and per-return thresholds, but each is bounded: the largest swing we found was a few thousand dollars. Over decades, the large gains come from moves that are already published. The one combination we could not find written up was paying the children in the same years as Roth conversions, which adds about 0.5%. Our guess is that anything Roth-sized and unpublished lives where value moves between entities and their owners: partnerships, S corporations and C corporations.
7. Most of this can now run automatically
“The long-term problem is how this kind of bug-hunting can be done routinely, e.g. as part of a test suite. Some approaches aren’t really automatable, because they require a lot of both human and AI agent work.”
What still needs a person is deciding what a finding means. Some cliffs are deliberate. Others are in the IRS’s forms, not in the law, whilst others come from the way the official text is compiled, yet:
- The text checks are routine. A new release is one download and one command, which fails on any fault the last release did not have, and git blame names the Act responsible.
- The model is a one-off cost. Once written and proved, it is a build: if a later change breaks a proved property, the build fails. When the law changes, only the changed provisions need rewriting.
- The comparison is routine once the input mappings are right. Getting them right was the hard part, and the same comparison found every mapping error.
Limits
- Coverage. The model covers the 2025 individual income tax for a household record. It leaves out the kiddie tax on the single-year return, 28% and §1250 gain, foreign income, passive activity rules, and most AMT preferences. The multi-year model holds 2025 law constant.
- Who checked what. The drafting triage and the reviews were done by AI agents against the official text, and have not been checked by a lawyer or CPA.
- Agreement is not truth. Where all four calculators agree they may share a misreading. The theorems and the IRS-example tests are the check on the model itself.
The code, the certificates, the comparison and the searches are in the lean-proofs folder of global-law.[3]

15:40:52 UTC
00:40:52 KST

PyPI's redesigned project pages with clearer security signals are now live
In July, we announced a series of planned updates to the PyPI user interface. Shaped by user research with members of the Python community, the updates aim to improve how we surface security signals and display package details.
We're pleased to share that this work is now complete and live on PyPI.
Context¶
With the rapid growth of the Python ecosystem, PyPI has become an increasingly attractive target for malicious actors. In response, the PyPI team has delivered many security features while taking a more active role in helping PyPI users assess and understand package security.
Digital attestations, introduced in 2024, are an example of this effort, providing a verifiable record of a release file's provenance (where it came from). However, provenance and attestations are still unfamiliar concepts for many PyPI users, and PyPI's interface did little to communicate their meaning, importance, or limitations.
To address this, PyPI partnered with Kabu Creative to bring clearer security signals to the PyPI UI, implementing the recommendations developed in the OpenSSF Attestations Style Guide.
Here's a look at what's changed, what it means for you, and what actions you can take.
The New Security Tab¶
The new security tab communicates release provenance states and displays per-file attestations, giving you a dedicated place to verify where the release files came from.
Provenance States¶
PyPI calculates each release's provenance state by checking whether each release file has an attestation. Typically, a release will be in one of two states:
Full Provenance¶
This means that 100% of the release files have attestations, carrying either or both of the supported attestation types:
PyPI Publish attestations confirm the file was published by a PyPI Trusted Publisher:
SLSA Build Provenance attestations link the file back to its exact source commit, build platform, and instructions:
You can review attestation metadata directly in the UI, or click on the transparency log link for more information.
No Provenance¶
"No provenance" simply means that no file in the release has an attestation. This is typical for older releases or projects that have not yet configured Trusted Publishing. No provenance does not necessarily indicate that a release or release file is malicious; it instead means that the file origin cannot be cryptographically verified.
When there is no provenance, you should rely on surrounding trust signals to assess security, such as maintainer history, release patterns, and repository activity. If you maintain the package, consider using Trusted Publishing with a publisher that generates attestations for your next release.
Provenance Warnings¶
Provenance warnings are designed to help you identify when a file may not have come from the project's maintainers, for example, if an attacker has taken over an account or a publishing workflow.
When provenance metadata looks unusual or conflicts with a project's history, PyPI surfaces a prominent warning. Many warnings have legitimate explanations, but each one is worth checking before you install.
Incomplete Provenance¶
This means that some files carry attestations while others do not (e.g., wheels were published via Trusted Publishing, but the source tarball was uploaded using an API token).
Exercise caution if you see this warning. Check release notes for build issues or ask in the project's issue tracker before installing.
Inconsistent Provenance¶
This warning indicates that files within the same release originate from different build systems or source repositories.
Check the attestation metadata to confirm whether all files came from sources controlled by the maintainers.
Change of Provenance¶
This warning tells you that the release files originated from a different repository or workflow than a previous release. While often legitimate (such as an organization name change or repository migration), it can also indicate a compromised pipeline or account takeover.
If you see this warning, check the project release notes or official channels to confirm that an infrastructure migration was planned.
Loss of Provenance¶
This means that a project that previously published with attestations has changed to uploading release files without them.
Check whether the maintainers intentionally changed their publishing process, for example by switching back to API tokens, or by changing their publishing configuration so that it no longer generates attestations. If this is unexpected, treat the release with skepticism until verified.
To learn more about provenance and attestations, including provenance warnings, see our user documentation.
Redesigned Project Detail Page¶
To support these changes, the project detail page has also been updated:
- Provenance warnings now appear at the top of the page, alongside status banners for yanked releases, archived projects, and pre-releases. If a project or release is quarantined, only the quarantine banner is shown, as it takes precedence over all other warnings and banners.
- When a release has full provenance or provenance warnings, its provenance state is displayed in the sidebar, linking through to the security tab for more information.
- Metadata is now ordered by utility and labeled as "PyPI data" or "Data verified by PyPI", replacing the old design where data was grouped and ordered by verification status.
Release Files and Release History¶
The redesigned release files tab helps you find the right file by searching and filtering long lists. You can also view the provenance state of each file within the table, and quickly identify late files, which are marked with an orange warning icon.
Late file warnings are shown when one or more files were uploaded more than 14 days after the initial release timestamp. These warnings only appear on releases prior to July 2026, when a policy was introduced to reject new files after 14 days.
The release history tab now also provides more details about each release, exposing file count, provenance state, and release status (i.e., quarantined, yanked, archived, or pre-release).
Getting Help¶
Alongside the interface updates, PyPI's user documentation has been extended to help you better navigate package security. Read our package security guide to learn about:
Looking Ahead & Acknowledgments¶
The new security tab gives PyPI a dedicated home for future attestation types and security notices. To help the wider open source ecosystem benefit from this work, we've also published a case study in the OpenSSF Attestations Style Guide detailing the design patterns and warnings developed during this project.
This initiative was funded by the Open Source Security Foundation and implemented by Kabu Creative. We would like to thank Dustin Ingram, Zach Steindler, and the members of the OpenSSF Securing Software Repositories Working Group for championing this effort. Special thanks to Mike Fiedler, Jacob Coffee, and members of the PyPI team for their thoughtful design feedback and code reviews, @cofiem for their contributions to the new release files tab, and our user interview participants and community testers for helping shape the final design.
Finally, we would like to thank the OpenSSF for its funding and continued partnership in securing the Python packaging ecosystem.

====================================================================================================

Australian STEM Videogame Challenge Canceled Due To AI (pcgamer.com) 56
			
		 	
				 PC Gamer  reports:
The Australian STEM Videogame Challenge, a "free national videogame development competition for students," has confirmed that the rise of generative AI means this year's event will be the last...
Speculation as to the cause of the program's cancellation focused primarily on the presence of AI in entries: Another self-proclaimed judge said the problem wasn't merely the use of AI in itself but that "some students just completely generate their games with AI...." 
[T]he Australian Council for Educational Research has confirmed that the cancellation was in fact due to AI.... "making it harder to judge what students made themselves, and moving the program away from its original purpose."   
Thanks to long-time Slashdot reader neoRUR for sharing the news.

Show HN: DeepSeek V3 in ~200 lines of pure Jax
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:04:15 UTC
00:04:15 KST

The architecture from the DeepSeek-V3 technical report in 190 lines of pure JAX, pretrained from scratch on a MacBook Air on public-domain detective fiction.
No Flax, no Equinox, no PyTorch. Params are plain dicts of arrays and every part of the model is a function you can read top to bottom in deepseek_v3.py.
Read the write-up: DeepSeek V3 in 190 lines of pure JAX, with diagrams, the code explained piece by piece, and the results.
One run of the default config on a MacBook Air M5 (24 GB):
With balancing on and off, validation loss at step 2000 was the same (3.921 vs 3.923). The bias evens out the experts without costing anything, which is the paper's claim.
Its own words, from the prompt "The fog came in off the":
The fog came in off the bay, and the great trees were spreading and the stars were shining.
This is an architecture demo, not a strong language model. See the write-up for what worked, what didn't, and what's missing.
The v1.0 release has the exact checkpoint and data from the published run. After installing (step 1):
mkdir -p data runs/main
curl -L -o runs/main/ckpt.pkl      https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax/releases/download/v1.0/ckpt.pkl          # 138 MB
curl -L -o data/tokenizer.json     https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax/releases/download/v1.0/tokenizer.json
.venv/bin/python sample.py --prompt "The fog came in off the" --num 3
The checkpoint is a Python pickle, which can run code when loaded. Only load checkpoints from sources you trust.
You need an Apple Silicon Mac, Python 3.12 and uv. The published run used 24 GB of memory. No Mac? See Running on CPU.
1. Install
git clone https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax.git && cd deepseek-v3-jax
uv venv -p 3.12 .venv
uv pip install --python .venv/bin/python -r requirements.txt
Every version in requirements.txt is pinned. jax-metal 0.1.1 only works with jax==0.4.34, so don't upgrade JAX.
2. Check your setup
JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu .venv/bin/pytest tests.py -q    # 39 passed
.venv/bin/python smoke_metal.py                   # optional: what jax-metal supports on your Mac
In smoke_metal.py, "grad through 2-axis contraction" fails on jax-metal 0.1.1. That's expected, and the model is written to avoid it.
3. Build the dataset
.venv/bin/python prepare_data.py
This downloads the 207 books in sources.txt (~120 MB, about 7 minutes), cleans them, trains an 8,192-token BPE tokenizer and writes data/train.bin, data/val.bin and data/tokenizer.json. It should print:
train: 211 docs, 83.4M chars, 21.69M tokens
val: 4 docs, 1.1M chars, 0.30M tokens
The published run's data had these hashes:
shasum -a 256 data/train.bin data/val.bin data/tokenizer.jsond37aca97e707bb81b6e4d266cc1adfd1f3e3556457c6eb724e495118a734e0bb  data/train.bin
b7ac285b115e6793b5d36aa5b4bc6e81a5fccfc7af0908a306d3828ebca9c31c  data/val.bin
c5a142b4006a967c880dda44ad792719d53c16d947da3b6c2332d6132d9967cc  data/tokenizer.json
You will most likely get different hashes. The Hammett books are pinned to exact Standard Ebooks commits and come out identical, but Project Gutenberg files can't be pinned, and Gutenberg has since re-issued 28 of the 203 books with small edits (mostly accents stripped, like "José" → "Jose"). The token counts come out the same, so training should behave the same.
To train on the exact same bytes, skip prepare_data.py and download the published data instead:
mkdir -p data
for f in train.bin val.bin tokenizer.json; do curl -L -o data/$f https://github.com/Vaibhaav-Tiwari/deepseek-v3-jax/releases/download/v1.0/$f; done
shasum -a 256 data/*.bin data/tokenizer.json    # should match the hashes above
4. Train
.venv/bin/python train.py                                         # the main run: 12k steps, ~2.6 h
.venv/bin/python train.py --steps 2000 --out runs/bias_on         # balancing ablation, with the bias
.venv/bin/python train.py --steps 2000 --out runs/bias_off --no-bias   # ...and without it
Each run writes metrics.jsonl (loss, validation loss, per-expert load, biases, tokens/s) and ckpt.pkl (saved at every evaluation) into its --out folder, which defaults to runs/main. Seeds are fixed, but GPU math on Metal isn't bit-for-bit deterministic, so expect your numbers to land close to the ones above rather than match them exactly.
5. Generate
.venv/bin/python sample.py --prompt "The fog came in off the" --num 3
Defaults: temperature 0.8, top-k 50, 300 new tokens per sample (--temp, --top-k, --n).
6. Make the figures
.venv/bin/python plots.py                    # needs runs/main, runs/bias_on, runs/bias_off
JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu .venv/bin/python charts.py # the rest of the write-up's charts
Both write into post/figures/.
To redraw the figures without training, download the logs from the release: metrics-main.jsonl, metrics-bias_on.jsonl and metrics-bias_off.jsonl go into runs/main/, runs/bias_on/ and runs/bias_off/ as metrics.jsonl. charts.py also needs the checkpoint and the data.
Everything runs without jax-metal. It's much slower, so use the smaller config:
JAX_PLATFORMS=cpu .venv/bin/python train.py --config small
--config small (4 layers, 8 experts, top-2, 128-token context) is meant for 8–16 GB Macs too. It has only been checked for a couple of steps, not trained to the end.
"190 lines" counts the non-blank, non-comment lines of deepseek_v3.py after its docstring:
tail -n +5 deepseek_v3.py | grep -cvE '^\s*(#|$)'    # 190
- Dense MoE compute. Every expert runs on every token and the unpicked ones are zeroed. The routing math is exact, but there's no sparse dispatch or expert parallelism.
- No weight absorption in MLA. The latent is decompressed explicitly, which is easier to read and slower at inference.
- Not included: FP8 training, DualPipe, node-limited routing, YaRN context extension, and any post-training.
- Scale. 36.2M parameters vs 671B, 6 layers vs 61, a 256-token context vs 128K.
All training text is public domain in the United States: Dashiell Hammett's Red Harvest, The Dain Curse, The Maltese Falcon and Continental Op stories (CC0 editions from Standard Ebooks), plus about 200 detective and crime novels from Project Gutenberg. Every source is listed in sources.txt. Public-domain status outside the US may differ.
DeepSeek-AI, DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report, 2024.
Code: MIT. The training text is public domain in the US (see above).

====================================================================================================

Chicken Normandy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
15:00:49 UTC
00:00:49 KST

Chicken Normandy
A cozy French chicken dinner with an irresistibly creamy apple and Dijon mustard sauce.
Serves4 to 6
Prep15 minutes
Cook45 minutes to 55 minutes
Why You’ll Love Chicken Normandy
- It comes together in just one pan. Sear juicy chicken thighs, sauté apple slices, and simmer a creamy sauce in a single skillet. It makes dinner prep and cleanup a breeze.
- The simple pan sauce is irresistible. The secret to a delicious pan sauce is capturing every morsel of flavor along the way. Lift the golden fond after browning the chicken with applejack, simmer with apple cider and Dijon mustard, then finish with tangy crème fraîche.
It’s that time of year that I’m finding excuses to sneak apples into every meal of the day. German apple pancakes stand in for a classic Dutch baby pancake, and strawberry spinach salad takes a backseat to one that’s made with apples and walnuts. There are countless ways to enjoy the crisp, sweet flavor of apples for dinner, but chicken Normandy is a cozy French chicken dinner that has become one of my favorites.
Chicken Normandy is a braised chicken dish made with fresh apples, apple cider, and apple brandy, each adding its own unique fruit flavor to the dish. The combination of apples with fresh thyme, zippy Dijon mustard, and tangy crème fraîche creates a creamy sauce that’s sweet, savory, and irresistibly delicious. Still, that’s not where the wins for this dinner stop. Chicken Normandy is made in a single skillet, so this dinner is a breeze to prepare. Just crisp the bacon and sear the chicken, make the sauce, and simmer until the chicken is juicy and apples are tender. Don’t forget to serve with a hunk of crusty bread to soak up every last drop of the sauce.
Straight from Our Recipe Tester
“This was everything I want in a Sunday dinner. It was unfussy to prepare, but the flavor is both deep and nuanced, savory, hearty, and warming. A perfect poem of a fall meal. I will definitely be making this again. I served this with potatoes, but it would also be great with a loaf of crusty bread to soak up the sauce.” —Andrea, October, 2026
Why You Should Trust Me
I studied culinary science to learn the hows and whys behind ingredients and cooking techniques. I’ve worked in test kitchens for the last 18 years developing and testing recipes for The Kitchn, cookbooks, and television. I love creating smart and delicious recipes that are worth repeating.
Key Ingredients in Chicken Normandy
- Chicken thighs: Bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs are juicy and flavorful. Choose thighs that are about the same size so they cook at the same rate.
- Applejack: This blended apple brandy is made from hard cider. Laird’s is a readily available brand of applejack, but Calvados or Cognac can be used instead.
- Apple cider: Apple cider has a complex, fresh apple flavor. If apple cider isn’t available, unfiltered apple juice makes an excellent substitute.
- Crème fraîche: Adds a creamy texture and tangy flavor to the apple-Dijon pan sauce. Find crème fraîche stocked alongside other specialty cheeses, or substitute with sour cream.
Chicken Normandy Recipe
A cozy French chicken dinner with an irresistibly creamy apple and Dijon mustard sauce.
Prep time 15 minutes
Cook time 45 minutes to 55 minutes
Serves 4 to 6
Nutritional Info
Ingredients
- 6 bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs (2 1/2 to 3 pounds)
- 1 1/2 teaspoons kosher salt, divided
- 1/4 teaspoon freshly ground black pepper
- 3 ounces sliced bacon (not thick cut), cut crosswise into 1/4-inch pieces
- 1 medium shallot, diced (3 tablespoons)
- 2 cloves garlic, minced
- 2 tablespoons all-purpose flour
- 1/2 cup applejack or Cognac
- 2 cups apple cider or unfiltered apple juice
- 5 sprigs fresh thyme, plus more for garnish
- 1 tablespoon Dijon mustard
- 1 medium sweet-tart apple, such as Pink Lady or Honeycrisp (8 ounces), cored and sliced 1/2-inch thick
- 2 tablespoons crème fraîche or sour cream
Instructions
- Pat 6 bone-in, skin-on chicken thighs dry with paper towels. Season all over with 1 teaspoon of the kosher salt and 1/4 teaspoon black pepper.
- Cook 3 ounces chopped bacon in a large 12-inch skillet over medium heat, stirring occasionally, until crisp and browned, about 5 minutes. Using a slotted spoon, transfer the bacon to a paper towel-lined plate.
- Add the chicken skin-side down to the skillet and sear undisturbed until the skin is very crisp and deep golden-brown, about 10 minutes. Flip and cook until the second side is just browned, 2 to 3 minutes more. Transfer to a plate skin-side up (the chicken will not be cooked through). Carefully pour off all but 2 tablespoons of the fat in the skillet.
- Add 1 diced medium shallot and 2 minced garlic cloves to the skillet. Cook over medium heat until translucent, about 1 minute. Add 2 tablespoons all-purpose flour and cook until no dry spots remain and a deeply browned film forms at the bottom of the skillet, about 1 minute.
- Add 1/2 cup applejack and simmer, scraping up any browned bits from the bottom of the skillet, until thickened and reduced, about 30 seconds. Stir in 2 cups apple cider, 5 fresh thyme sprigs, 1 tablespoon Dijon mustard, and the remaining 1/2 teaspoon kosher salt. Bring to a simmer.
- Return the chicken skin-side up to the skillet and add any accumulated juices on the plate. Add 1 sliced medium apple and simmer until the sauce thickens slightly, the apples are crisp-tender, and an instant-read thermometer inserted into the thickest part of the chicken registers at least 165°F, 20 to 22 minutes.
- Turn off the heat. Remove and discard the thyme sprigs. Stir 2 tablespoons crème fraîche into the sauce. Garnish with the bacon and fresh thyme leaves if desired
Recipe Notes
Storage: Refrigerate leftovers in an airtight container for up to 4 days.

Why Cuisinart’s 2-Quart Pour Saucepan Makes Boiling and Draining So Much Easier

The smaller holes are great for little things like pastina, grains, orzo, and lentils, so nothing slips through. Demystifying phone unlocking tools: A technical overview
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
14:44:28 UTC
23:44:28 KST

This post is a written description of a presentation titled Phone unlocking tools and where to find them that we have delivered privately to different events and organizations, including Primavera Hacker 25, the Freedom of the Press Foundation, and the Public Interest Technology Group. It provides a technical overview of how commercial forensic tools compromise mobile devices, focusing on the specific attack surfaces exploited at each stage of the device lifecycle. Further posts will focus on sample analysis, and defenses. See also the previous posts of this serie:
Introduction #
Commercial phone unlocking tools are not magic. They exploit the same classes of vulnerabilities that security researchers publish about at conferences and in academic papers. What makes them distinctive is not the novelty of the techniques, but the systematic commercialization of exploitation: acquiring or developing exploits for each hardware and software combination, and reaching a certain degree of stability, packaging them into automated workflows that require minimal operator expertise.
This post aims to demystify the technical foundations of these tools. The attack vectors available to a forensic examiner with physical access to a device are constrained by well-understood boundaries: the boot chain, the trusted execution environment, the secure element (when present), and the kernel’s peripheral driver stack. This post mostly details Android internals, however iOS equivalent concepts are technically similar.
Much of the background research referenced here comes from Quarkslab, whose extensive publications on TEEs, and Android’s data encryption architecture have been invaluable to understand these tools. Their work, alongside reporting from Amnesty International’s Security Lab and other NGOs, and analysis by the GrapheneOS project, provides the empirical basis for the claims made in this post.
Hardware security architecture #
Before examining attack vectors, it is necessary to understand the hardware security architecture of modern Android devices. The following sections describe the components that forensic tools must eventually hack or bypass.
The System-on-Chip: Normal World and Secure World #
Modern ARM-based mobile processors implement TrustZone, a hardware security extension that partitions the processor into two execution environments: the Normal World and the Secure World. The Normal World runs the Android operating system, user applications, and the Linux kernel. The Secure World runs a separate, minimal operating system, the Trusted OS, along with small, specialized applications known as Trusted Applications (TAs).
The two worlds share the same physical processor cores but are hardware-isolated: the Normal World cannot directly read or write Secure World memory. Communication between the two happens through a monitor, a piece of code running at the highest privilege level (EL3 on ARMv8), which handles context switches triggered by Secure Monitor Call (SMC) instructions. Each physical processor core effectively provides two virtual cores, with the Non-Secure bit in the Secure Configuration Register determining which world is active at any given time.
The Trusted OS hosts critical security services. Two are directly relevant to device unlocking:
- Gatekeeper: responsible for verifying user credentials (PIN, password, pattern). It implements rate-limiting to throttle brute-force attempts and, upon successful authentication, issues a signed authentication token.
- Keymaster: the key management service that stores and operates on cryptographic keys. Some keys are authentication-bound, meaning they can only be used after Gatekeeper has issued a valid token.
Different SoC vendors ship different TrustZone implementations. Google (and AOSP in general) uses TrustyOS; Qualcomm uses QSEE; Samsung uses TEEGRIS (on both Exynos and MediaTek SoCs in Samsung devices); Huawei uses TrustedCore; Trustonic’s Kinibi is also often found on MediaTek and Exynos SoCs. The security properties vary between implementations.
The Secure Element #
A Secure Element (SE) is a physically separate, tamper-resistant hardware chip that provides an additional layer of security beyond the TEE. Unlike the Secure World, which runs on the same SoC as the Normal World, a Secure Element is an independent processor with its own boot chain, its own firmware, and its own cryptographic key storage.
In the Android ecosystem, the Secure Element is exposed through the StrongBox API (introduced in Android 9). Google’s Pixel phones, starting with the Pixel 3, include the Titan M chip as their Secure Element, that was later upgraded with the Titan M2 from Pixel 6 onward. On Apple devices, the equivalent is the Secure Enclave Processor (SEP).
The Secure Element’s role in device encryption is to hold key-encryption keys and enforce rate-limiting for credential verification in hardware that the main SoC cannot tamper with, even if fully compromised. This is an important distinction: on a device without a Secure Element, compromising the TEE is sufficient to extract or bypass credential-derived keys. On a device with a Secure Element, the attacker must additionally compromise a separate, hardened chip.
Unfortunately, only flagship devices typically include a Secure Element. Most mid-range and budget devices rely solely on the TEE for credential protection. This architectural gap is the single most important factor determining the chances that a device has to resist forensic unlocking when powered off.
The boot chain #
The boot chain establishes a chain of trust from immutable hardware to the running operating system. Each stage verifies the integrity of the next before executing it.
The chain proceeds as follows:
- Boot ROM: the first code executed at power-on. It is burned into silicon at manufacture and is, in principle, immutable and unpatchable (with a few modern exceptions to deploy patches for vulnerabilities). The Boot ROM verifies and loads the next stage (the preloader or secondary bootloader). It forms the hardware root of trust.
- Preloader/Secondary Bootloader (BL2): loaded and verified by the Boot ROM. On MediaTek SoCs, this is often called the preloader. It initializes hardware, loads the Trusted OS into the Secure World, and loads the main bootloader into the Normal World.
- Trusted OS: loaded into the Secure World by the preloader. It hosts Gatekeeper, Keymaster, and other Trusted Applications. Its integrity is verified by the preloader as part of the secure boot chain.
- Bootloader (BL3): the Normal World bootloader (e.g., Little Kernel on MediaTek, ABL on Qualcomm). It enforces Android Verified Boot (AVB), anti-rollback protections, and device-state checks before loading the Android kernel.
- Android OS: the Linux kernel and userspace. Verified Boot (dm-verity) ensures the integrity of system partitions at runtime.
When a Secure Element is present, it runs its own independent boot chain in parallel: Boot ROM → Bootrom EXT → Bootloader → OS. The Secure Element’s boot chain is entirely separate from the SoC’s, providing the most isolation.
If any stage in the boot chain is compromised, all subsequent stages are untrustworthy. This is why Boot ROM vulnerabilities are so devastating: they are often unpatchable, they undermine everything built on top, and they affect every device using the vulnerable silicon for its entire hardware lifetime.
Android data encryption #
Understanding the attack vectors requires understanding what the attacker is ultimately trying to obtain: the decryption keys for the user’s data.
Full Disk Encryption vs. File-Based Encryption #
Android has used two encryption schemes over its history:
Full Disk Encryption (FDE) encrypts the entire data partition with a single key, derived from the user’s credentials. The key must be available before the OS can boot, so the user is prompted for their PIN or password at boot time. FDE was the default until Android 6 and has been deprecated since Android 13.
File-Based Encryption (FBE) encrypts individual files with different keys, allowing more granular access control. FBE has been the default since Android 7 and is required since Android 10. It divides storage into two classes:
- Device Encrypted (DE) storage: available immediately after boot, before the user authenticates. Used for system-critical functionality (alarms, phone dialer, Direct Boot). The DE key is derived without user credentials.
- Credential Encrypted (CE) storage: available only after the user authenticates for the first time after boot. This is where all user data resides—messages, photos, application data. The CE key is derived from the user’s credentials, stretched through scrypt, and protected by keys held in the TEE (and, when present, the Secure Element).
CE key derivation: The role of credentials #
Quarkslab’s Android Data Encryption in depth provides the most detailed public analysis of how the CE key is derived. The process, in simplified form, proceeds as follows:
- The user’s credentials (PIN, password, or pattern) are stretched using scrypt, a memory-hard key derivation function, with parameters and salt stored in DE-protected files under /data/system_de/<uid>/spblob. The scrypt step is intentionally slow: it introduces a negligible delay for a single authentication attempt but makes brute-forcing computationally expensive.
- The stretched credentials are combined with other material to form a password blob, which is sent to the Gatekeeper Trusted Application in the TEE. Gatekeeper verifies the blob against a stored password handle using an HMAC with an internal key. If verification succeeds, Gatekeeper issues a signed authentication token.
- The authentication token is presented to Keymaster, which uses it to unlock an authentication-bound key. This key is used to perform the first decryption of the Synthetic Password, an intermediate secret stored in encrypted form on the filesystem.
- The Synthetic Password is decrypted a second time using a key derived from the user’s credentials (the applicationId). This second decryption uses AES-GCM, which provides authenticated encryption: if the wrong credentials are supplied, the GCM tag will not match and decryption will fail. This is the cryptographic check that ultimately binds the CE key to the correct credentials.
- From the Synthetic Password, the system derives the CE file encryption keys used by the Linux kernel’s fscrypt subsystem to decrypt individual files.
The critical observation is that user credentials are cryptographically embedded in the key derivation chain. There is no way to bypass them purely through software attacks: an attacker who compromises the TEE can bypass the authentication token check, but they still need to brute-force the credentials through scrypt to derive the correct key material.
Key derivation with a Secure Element (Weaver) #
On devices with a Secure Element, the key derivation process includes an additional step. Instead of relying solely on Gatekeeper in the TEE, the system uses Weaver, a service running on the Secure Element. Weaver stores a secret value that is released only upon correct credential verification and enforces its own independent rate-limiting.
This means that even if an attacker fully compromises the TEE, or other Trusted Applications as shown by Synacktiv’s Kinibi TEE blogpost, they cannot extract the Weaver secret or bypass its throttling, if implemented properly. Brute-forcing must proceed online, at the rate the Secure Element permits, which, for a device like the Pixel with Titan M, means exponential backoff.
Attack vectors #
With the architecture established, we can now map the specific attack surfaces that forensic tools exploit. These fall into two main categories, corresponding to teo device states: powered off or powered on but never unlocked (BFU), and powered on and previously unlocked (AFU).
BFU attacks on devices without a Secure Element #
The most complete form of BFU attack targets the boot chain itself. If an attacker can compromise the Boot ROM or the preloader, they can break the entire chain of trust, including the TEE.
The attack proceeds as follows:
- Boot ROM exploitation: the attacker connects to the device via USB and exploits a vulnerability in the Boot ROM (or uses a vendor-specific download/recovery mode, such as MediaTek’s Download Mode or Qualcomm’s EDL mode) to gain code execution before the bootloader runs.
- Boot chain patching: with Boot ROM-level access, the attacker patches or replaces the preloader to disable secure boot verification of subsequent stages. This allows loading a modified Trusted OS and modified Trusted Applications.
- TEE patching: the attacker replaces or patches Gatekeeper to accept any credentials and issue valid authentication tokens regardless of input. They also patch or extract keys from Keymaster. As Quarkslab demonstrated in their proof-of-concept on Samsung A22 devices (MT6769V and MT6833V SoCs), this involved patching Samsung’s TEEGRIS operating system: first disabling verification of the root filesystem, then disabling TA signature verification, and finally patching Gatekeeper’s credential comparison to always succeed.
- Key material extraction: with a rooted Android system and a compromised TEE, the attacker extracts the encrypted Synthetic Password and the intermediate decryption result from Keymaster.
- Offline brute force: the attacker now has all the material needed for offline brute-forcing. The brute-force loop is: 
  - Generate a candidate password
  - Stretch it through scrypt with the stored parameters
  - Derive the applicationId
  - Attempt AES-GCM decryption of the Synthetic Password
  - If the GCM tag matches, the correct credentials have been found
Because this brute-force runs entirely offline, on the attacker’s hardware, it is bounded only by computational resources and password complexity. A numeric PIN of six digits or fewer is trivially recoverable. Even longer numeric PINs fall quickly on dedicated hardware. Only a strong alphanumeric and symbols password provides meaningful resistance.
MediaTek devices are particularly vulnerable to this attack. Multiple MediaTek SoCs contain Boot ROM vulnerabilities that are publicly known and exploited by open-source tools such as MTKClient. Because Boot ROM code is immutable, these vulnerabilities cannot be patched: every device using the affected silicon is permanently compromised, regardless of Android version or security patch level. Quarkslab’s proof-of-concept targeted exactly this class of device, demonstrating the full chain from Boot ROM exploit to CE key recovery.
BFU attacks on devices with a Secure Element #
When a Secure Element is present, the attack chain is fundamentally harder. Even if the attacker achieves everything described above, they still cannot extract the Weaver secret from the Secure Element, under normal circumstances. Chances of a Secure Element exploit are pretty low, especially as being distributed as part of a widely available forensics commercial suite.
As anticipated, the Secure Element runs its own independent boot chain on physically separate silicon. Its keys cannot be extracted by the main SoC, and its rate-limiting logic is enforced in hardware that the attacker does not control.
The result is that brute-force can often only proceed online: each attempt must query the Secure Element, which enforces some kind of backoff. This slows down the attack significantly. A ten-digit PIN that falls in seconds to offline brute-force may take a long time against a properly implemented Secure Element. An alphanumeric password becomes likely unbreakable.
This is why, according to Cellebrite’s own February 2025 support matrix, brute-force capabilities are generally not available for Pixel devices with Titan M. The Secure Element is doing exactly what it was designed to do.
That said, the Secure Element is not immune to vulnerabilities. Quarkslab demonstrated code execution on the Titan M chip through CVE-2022-20233, a single-byte out-of-bounds write in the Keymaster task that could be exploited to hijack execution flow. This vulnerability has since been patched, but it demonstrates that Secure Elements, while vastly more resistant than TEE-only configurations, can still have issues. Google is offering up to $1,500,000 for a zero-click vulnerabilities on the Pixel Titan M2.
AFU attacks: The lock screen is just UI #
After the first unlock, the threat model fully changes. In AFU state, the CE decryption keys are loaded in memory and remain there. The lock screen is no longer a cryptographic barrier, it is just a user interface element, a screen overlay that prevents interaction but does not re-encrypt data.
This means that an AFU attacker does not need to brute-force credentials at all. They need to achieve code execution in the kernel or a privileged process, at which point they have direct access to the decrypted filesystem. The lock screen is bypassed, and no encryption needs to be broken.
The primary attack surface in AFU state is the USB interface. When a device is locked but in AFU state, the USB controller remains active and exposes the device’s kernel to a substantial attack surface.
As the diagram illustrates, a peripheral emulator connected to a locked device’s USB port can interact with the kernel through approximately 200 reachable drivers, including MTP (Media Transfer Protocol), MSC (Mass Storage Class), HID (Human Interface Device), Audio, and CVC (Communication Voice Class) subsystems. Each of these drivers is a potential attack surface for memory corruption vulnerabilities.
The exploit used by Cellebrite against a Serbian activist’s device in December 2024, as documented by Amnesty International, attempted to use at least three Linux kernel USB driver vulnerabilities:
- CVE-2024-53104: a vulnerability in the USB Video Class (UVC) driver.
- CVE-2024-53197: an out-of-bounds write in the ALSA USB-audio driver, triggered when a malicious USB device provides a bNumConfigurations value exceeding allocated memory. This vulnerability affects legacy code present since Linux kernel 2.6.26 (2008) and was confirmed by CISA to be actively exploited in the wild.
- CVE-2024-50302: a memory leak in the HID subsystem that exposes uninitialized kernel memory, including encryption keys and authentication tokens, when processing crafted USB HID reports.
The Cellebrite Turbo Link device, a specialized hardware adapter that connects between the forensic workstation and the target device, likely functions as a peripheral emulator capable of presenting itself as multiple USB device types in rapid succession, probing for each vulnerability to achieve kernel-level code execution.
Many of these USB drivers are loaded by default and remain reachable even when the device is locked. The kernel does not distinguish between a legitimate USB accessory and a malicious peripheral emulator, thus this is the main weakness that AFU exploitation often relies upon.
AFU attacks on locked devices in practice #
Once the attacker achieves kernel code execution through USB driver exploitation, the lock screen is irrelevant. The attacker can:
- Directly read the decrypted filesystem, since CE keys are already in memory
- Perform a Full File System (FFS) extraction of all user data
- Access application data, messages, photos, credentials, and metadata
- Potentially install persistent backdoors
As we already knew, a device in AFU state is fundamentally less secure than a device in BFU state, regardless of the strength of the user’s password or the presence of a Secure Element. The Secure Element protects credential-derived keys at boot; it does not protect already-decrypted data in memory after the first unlock.
This is confirmed by Cellebrite’s February 2025 support matrix, which shows AFU extraction capabilities even for devices (including recent Pixels with stock Android) that resist BFU attacks. Notably, according to the same documentation, GrapheneOS on Pixel devices resists AFU extraction, probably thanks to the attack surface reduction and the USB restrictions that are implemented beyond stock Android.
A note on iOS: checkm8 and USB Restricted Mode #
Apple devices face analogous attack surfaces. The checkm8 exploit, publicly released in 2019, targets an unpatchable Boot ROM vulnerability present in all Apple devices from the iPhone 4S through the iPhone X (A5 through A11 SoCs). Like MediaTek Boot ROM vulnerabilities, checkm8 cannot be fixed through software updates: every affected device is permanently exploitable.
For newer Apple devices, AFU attacks rely on USB-based exploitation. Apple introduced USB Restricted Mode in iOS 11.4.1 to mitigate this: if the device has been locked for more than one hour, the Lightning or USB-C port is restricted to charging only, disabling data connections that forensic tools require.
However, Quarkslab’s analysis of CVE-2025-24200 (reported by CitizenLab) revealed that USB Restricted Mode was bypassable. The vulnerability, reported by Citizen Lab and patched in iOS 18.3.1, allowed a physical attacker to re-enable USB data connections on a locked device by exploiting a flaw in the Accessibility framework. The profiled daemon, which handles device management settings, failed to check whether the device was locked before processing requests to disable USB Restricted Mode. In other words, the USB port was only soft-disabled: something as simple as a logic bug in the policy enforcement allowed it to be re-enabled.
Forensic vendors have already started producing countermeasures to Apple’s inactivity reboot. Cellebrite’s Spring 2026 release for Inseyets advertises a “Safeguard Mode” that “mitigates the impact of iOS inactivity reboot timers by preserving access to a device” — in practice, preventing the device from returning to BFU state once they have AFU access. Magnet Forensics ships a hardware product called GrayKey Preserve which similarly aims to suppress the auto-reboot, network wipes and remote wipes on captured devices. These tools likely ship the same AFU exploits in a different, locked-down chassis that performs only preservation rather than extraction, which is also why they can move faster than the full extraction suites.
Defensive implications #
The attack vectors described above lead to a clear set of defensive priorities.
BFU is your best defense #
The most impactful single action when facing potential device seizure is to power the device off. This returns it to BFU state, where:
- CE storage is encrypted and keys are not in memory
- USB driver exploitation cannot yield decrypted data
- The attacker must compromise the boot chain and brute-force credentials
- On devices with a Secure Element, brute-force is rate-limited by hardware
Enable Lockdown Mode on iOS #
iOS users should enable Lockdown Mode. It hardens the device against exploitation across messaging, web, and attachments, and there is public-record evidence of it stopping at least one extraction attempt: an FBI CART court filing on an iPhone 13 explicitly states that “Because the iPhone was in Lockdown mode, CART could not extract that device”. The mechanism is not officially documented, but it is consistent with what Lockdown Mode is known to do: shrink the reachable attack surface, restrict USB more aggressively, and block the configuration-profile and MDM enrollment that forensic and “jailbreak” tools have historically abused to sideload unverified components.
Auto-reboot: returning to BFU automatically #
If a device cannot be manually powered off, an automatic reboot mechanism provides the next best defense. After a configurable period without user authentication, the device reboots, returning to BFU state and purging decryption keys from memory.
GrapheneOS has implemented this feature for years. Apple introduced it in iOS 18. Stock Android on Pixel devices gained a limited version in Android 15, but its implementation remains less configurable and less aggressive than GrapheneOS’s. The feature is the most useful if the timeout is short (2-4 hours) and loses its value when its multiple days, as Google enforces likely as a result of negotiation with law enforcement. GrapheneOS in particular allows the timeout to be configured very low, precisely because the team has long considered the risk of an exploit being produced to inhibit the reboot itself.
USB port restriction #
Disabling USB data transfer when the device is locked directly eliminates the AFU attack surface described above. Android 15 introduces USB restriction options; iOS has had USB Restricted Mode since version 11. GrapheneOS provides more trustworthy and configurable USB restriction.
Password strength #
The brute-force analysis makes the case for strong passwords unambiguous:
- A 4-digit PIN: trivially crackable offline, and crackable even online within hours to days
- A 6-digit PIN: trivially crackable offline; crackable online within days to weeks depending on rate-limiting implementation
- A pattern: equivalent to or weaker than a short PIN in entropy
- An alphanumeric password of 10+ characters with mixed case and symbols: computationally unlikely to brute-force offline through scrypt; almost impossible to brute-force online against a Secure Element
The password is only required at boot. Biometric authentication (fingerprint, face recognition) handles daily unlocking, unless the user is in a jurisdiction where physical coercion is likely, whether lawfully or not. The marginal inconvenience of entering a strong password once after each reboot is minimal compared to the protection it provides.
Device choice #
According to Cellebrite’s own February 2025 documentation:
- MediaTek-based devices: effectively no mitigation possible against BFU attacks due to unpatchable Boot ROM vulnerabilities.
- Most non-Pixel, non-Samsung Android devices: considered unlockable, with few exceptions, due to a combination of delayed security updates, weak TEE implementations, and absent Secure Elements.
- Samsung devices (Exynos): partial protection, varying by model and patch level.
- Google Pixel with stock Android: resistant to BFU attacks on recent models, but AFU file system extraction is possible.
- Google Pixel with GrapheneOS: resistant to both BFU and AFU attacks on recent models (6a and newer). This is the strongest protection available on any Android device.
A subsequent Cellebrite matrix leaked in October 2025 suggests Cellebrite has since also lost Full File System extraction support against unlocked GrapheneOS devices.
It is worth noting that alternative Android distributions such as LineageOS or CalyxOS, while valuable for other reasons, do not meaningfully change the forensic unlocking picture from the stock or vendor ROM.
Application-level encryption #
Applications that implement their own encryption layer with a separate password can provide defense-in-depth against full device compromise. Password managers’ master password vaults are a good example: even if the entire device filesystem is extracted, the vault remains encrypted under a key that the forensic tool has not obtained.
However, the effectiveness of application-level protection depends on several factors: whether the app actually implements its own encryption independently from the OS credential protection, whether decryption keys remain in memory after first unlock (making them vulnerable to AFU extraction), whether the app relies on the system biometric authentication (which, without a Secure Element, is broken along with the rest of the OS key hierarchy), and whether notifications or message previews are cached in plaintext outside the app’s encrypted storage.
Conclusion #
The way these tools operate is not magic nor secret. The more we collect samples, reverse engineer and read promotional and support material, the better we understand capabilities and defenses.
As we said many times these tools simply shouldn’t commercially exist. The companies that build these tools are active participants in the zero-day vulnerability trade, stockpiling security flaws that weaken every device, for every user, everywhere. The technical defenses described here are effective but place the burden on individuals and communities.
Update (20 May 2026): following feedback from Final of the GrapheneOS Foundation, this post was updated with information about iOS Lockdown Mode, forensic-vendor countermeasures to iOS inactivity reboot (Cellebrite Safeguard Mode and Magnet GrayKey Preserve), GrapheneOS’s auto-reboot design rationale, and the October 2025 Cellebrite matrix leak.
References #
- Quarkslab, “Android Data Encryption in depth” (2023)
- Quarkslab, “Introduction to Trusted Execution Environment: ARM’s TrustZone” (2018)
- Quarkslab, “A Deep Dive into Samsung’s TrustZone” (series)
